```
36	   On pickup, move the `## Todo` item's text into
37	   `## In Progress` so the section is self-describing (not
38	   a bare ladder), and capture it in the chores opener so

40	   close-out remove it and add the `## Done` one-liner.

44	2. **single-field `options_flags` leaves → `value` field.**
45	   `0.47.0` introduced the convention (single-field leaf names
46	   its field `value`, declares the flag via `#[arg(long = "…")]`,
47	   so consumers read `args.<leaf>.value` not `args.<leaf>.<leaf>`)
48	   on the new `squash` leaf. Sweep the pre-existing single-field
49	   leaves to match: `repo`, `dry_run`, `private`, `account`,

56	3. **Investigate `linkme` for subcommand registration.**
57	   Distributed-slice registry — each subcommand registers itself
58	   at link time; `main.rs` discovers them via the slice rather
59	   than matching a `Commands` enum. Reduces per-subcommand
60	   touchpoints from 3 (mod decl + enum variant + match arm) to 1
61	   (registration). Costs: loses compile-time exhaustiveness
62	   (missing registration = runtime gap); help-output ordering
63	   depends on link order unless sorted; macro-magic dependency.
64	   Revisit once the `0.50.0` trait sweep's per-arm cost has been
65	   felt under real "add a subcommand" load.
66	   <https://github.com/dtolnay/linkme>

68	   shape as `linkme` — runtime-iterable registry populated by

70	   linkme's. Pick one if/when the trait sweep's match becomes
71	   the felt bottleneck.

269	## Bugs
270	
271	   Known defects we're aware of but haven't scheduled a fix
272	   for. Each entry should describe what goes wrong, when,
273	   and the cost of the failure. Numbering convention same as
274	   `## Todo` (manual `1.` `2.` …).
275	
276	1. **`finalize::surface_previous_failures` is racy and
277	   bounded by "next vc-x1 run".** The current model has
278	   four gaps:
279	   - **Stale forever.** Markers sit on disk until the
280	     next `vc-x1 <anything>` runs. If the user abandons
281	     the workspace (e.g., CI / scheduled use), failures
282	     are never surfaced.
283	   - **Concurrent surface_previous_failures.** Two
284	     `vc-x1` runs racing: both `read_dir`, both print
285	     the marker, one deletes (the other's `remove_file`
286	     silently fails). The user sees the same failure
287	     printed twice.
288	   - **Mid-write torn read.** A `finalize --exec` child
289	     writing a marker while a sibling `vc-x1` is
290	     surfacing could read partial content. Atomic-rename
291	     on write would close this.
292	   - **No notify-at-failure path.** A detached
293	     `finalize --exec` failure only becomes visible when
294	     the user next runs *any* `vc-x1` command — fine for
295	     interactive use, invisible for CI / scheduled use
296	     where there may be no next run.
297	
298	   The exec-child gate in `main.rs` (since 0.52.0-3)
299	   patches one related case — the detached child eating
300	   its own prior markers before the user can see them —
301	   but doesn't address any of the above. Holistic fix
302	   needs locking + atomic writes + maybe a
303	   notify-at-failure path for hands-off use.
304

308	and older `## Done` sections are moved to [done.md](done.md) to keep this file small.
```

```
1	# Architecture
2	
3	How the `vc-x1` binary is structured: the clap-aware CLI layer
4	and the (emerging) clap-free subcommand layer. This is the
5	"what + some why" — generic and slow-moving. The *how* and the
6	per-cycle / per-subcommand status live in `notes/chores/chores-*.md`
7	and `notes/todo.md`.
8	
9	This is a living document; the app is under continuous
10	development and the repo will keep changing.
11	
12	## Overview
13	
14	`vc-x1` is a single binary that supports subcommands,
15	`vc-x1 <subcommand>`, for managing a jj-git **dual-repo workspace**
16	with an app repo plus a `.claude` bot-session repo. The commits
17	cross-reference each other via `ochid:` trailers at the bottom
18	of each commit message. See [`README.md`](README.md) for the
19	user-facing picture and [`CLAUDE.md`](CLAUDE.md) for the bot
20	workflow.
21	
22	## Two layers: CLI args vs subcommand Context + Params
23	
24	Goal: separate clap-aware argument parsing from subcommand
25	operation logic, so a future front-end (TUI, library embedding)
26	can call the same core without dragging clap along. The trigger
27	was `args.account.account`-style nested-leaf access leaking into
28	subcommand bodies; accessor shortcuts were rejected as hiding
29	the mismatch rather than fixing it.
30	
31	- **CLI layer (clap-aware)**: builds `XxxParams` from the
32	  user-facing `XxxArgs` `#[derive(Args)]` clap struct.
33	  Options/flags used in more than one subcommand live as
34	  flattened leaves/bundles in `src/options_flags/`.
35	- **Subcommand layer (clap-free)**: entrypoint shape
36	  `fn x(ctx: &Context, params: &XxxParams) -> Result<…>` —
37	  isolates the subcommand from clap and from the user.
38	  - `Context` — platform state shared by every subcommand.
39	  - `XxxParams` — that subcommand's inputs, derived from
40	    config / command-line args.
41	- **Boundary conversion**: `XxxArgs → XxxParams` via
42	  `impl From<&XxxArgs>` or `impl TryFrom<&XxxArgs>`, depending
43	  on whether the conversion can fail.
44	
45	Two parameters per subcommand; the signature documents what the
46	subcommand depends on. `Context` is the same across all
47	subcommands; `XxxParams` carry the per-subcommand inputs.
48	
49	Rules for the subcommand layer (some still aspirational — see
50	[the Context+Params port](#args--context--params)):
51	
52	1. Plain options, flat fields, `Default` — no clap types in
53	   `XxxParams`. Domain types (`RepoSelector`, `Scope`) are
54	   fine; leaf wrappers (`RepoOption`) are not.
55	2. Typed errors (`enum XError`, not `Box<dyn Error>`) — a TUI
56	   matches variants to pick dialogs; CLI formats them.
57	3. Returned outcomes, not `println!` — each subcommand returns
58	   a structured result; CLI formats it, a library writes
59	   nothing to stdout itself.
60	4. Progress via an optional `&mut dyn ProgressSink` on
61	   long-running subcommands — CLI installs a stderr sink, tests
62	   a recording sink.
63	5. No globals, no implicit cwd/env reads in subcommands — CLI
64	   resolves cwd/env once at startup and builds `Context`.
65	
66	None of this is sacred — the whole structure is an experiment.
67	The current scope is deliberately narrow: a small `Context`
68	(two params, not one god blob); no trait-based DI yet; one
69	crate, not a workspace split; `src/options_flags/` leaves left
70	in place — only their consumers change shape. Widen any of it
71	when the evidence (a second front-end, a second consumer crate,
72	a real platform-surface divergence) calls for it — not before.
73	
74	### Naming
75	
76	Earlier drafts of this design used `Workspace` / `XOptions`;
77	the implementation (0.44.0) chose `Context` / `XxxParams`:
78	
79	- `Workspace` → `Context` — Cargo owns "workspace" formally,
80	  and this codebase already uses "workspace" for the dual-repo
81	  project root (`find_workspace_root`).
82	- `XOptions` → `XxxParams` — avoids visual collision with
83	  `Option<T>` (a params struct mixes required and
84	  `Option<T>`-typed fields) and with `src/options_flags/`.
85	
86	## Module map
87	
88	The *kinds* of module and what they're for — not an exhaustive
89	catalog (read the `//!` docstrings; `notes/chores/chores-*.md` for
90	per-subcommand refactor status):
91	
92	**CLI layer:**
93	
94	- `src/main.rs` — the clap edge: `Cli` (`Parser`), `Commands`
95	  (`Subcommand`) + the dispatch arms, `cli_with_banner()` (the
96	  per-subcommand `vc-x1 X.Y.Z` banner), `CompleteEnv` wiring,
97	  and `bm_track` (the permanent `main`-bookmark tracking
98	  diagnostic on every command's entry/exit).
99	- `src/options_flags/` — reusable `#[derive(Args)]` *leaves*
100	  (one flag, or a small fixed pair) and *bundles*
101	  (`#[command(flatten)]` of N leaves) shared across
102	  subcommands, so a flag is defined (parser, help, completer)
103	  exactly once. See
104	  [`src/options_flags/README.md`](src/options_flags/README.md).
105	- `src/common.rs` — helpers the read-only commit-query
106	  subcommands (`chid` / `desc` / `list` / `show`) share:
107	  `for_each_repo`, `collect_ids`, `resolve_revset`, the
108	  `DotSpec` / `..`-notation parsing, `Header` /
109	  `resolve_header`, the `format_*` printers, and the
110	  `-R`/`-s` resolution stack — `find_workspace_root` /
111	  `default_scope` / `scope_to_repos` / `resolve_repos`
112	  (the top-level entry combining the `-R PATH` override and
113	  the `-s code|bot|code,bot` role selection).
114	
115	**Subcommand-layer scaffolding:**
116	
117	- `src/context.rs` — `Context`, the shared platform handle
118	  (today: the loaded `UserConfig` + the `--log` path; built
119	  once at startup via `Context::load(log)`).
120	- `src/config.rs` — `UserConfig` (`~/.config/vc-x1/config.toml`).
121	- `src/options_flags/scope.rs` — `Scope` (a newtype over
122	  `Vec<Side>` — the `code`/`bot` role set) and `parse_scope`.
123	
124	**Subcommand modules** — `src/<x>.rs` holds the subcommand's
125	`XxxArgs` (`#[derive(Args)]`, composing `options_flags/` leaves
126	where one exists) and the entrypoint `pub fn x(...)`. Modules:
127	`chid`, `desc` (+ `desc_helpers`), `list`, `show`,
128	`validate_desc`, `fix_desc`, `validate_todo` / `fix_todo`
129	(+ `todo_helpers`), `clone`, `init` (+ `init/params`),
130	`symlink`, `sync`, `finalize`, `push` (a resumable state
131	machine). Which ones have ported to `(ctx, params)` and which
132	compose `options_flags/` leaves: `notes/chores/chores-*.md` +
133	`notes/todo.md`.
134	
135	**Support:**
136	
137	- `src/repo_utils.rs`, `src/url.rs`, `src/toml_simple.rs`,
138	  `src/logging.rs` — jj/repo helpers, URL parsing, a minimal
139	  TOML reader, the CLI logger.
140	- `src/test_helpers.rs`, `src/test_tmp_root.rs` —
141	  `#[cfg(test)]` fixtures and tempdir resolution. Subcommand
142	  modules also carry their own `#[cfg(test)] mod tests` (and
143	  `integration_tests` for `sync` / `push`).
144	
145	## Subcommand model
146	
147	Adding a subcommand `x`:
148	
149	1. Create `src/x.rs` with `pub struct XxxArgs`
150	   (`#[derive(Args)]`), composing leaves from
151	   `src/options_flags/` where one exists.
152	2. Add a variant `X(x::XxxArgs)` to `Commands` in `src/main.rs`
153	   with a `///` summary (and `#[command(long_about = …)]` for
154	   the long form).
155	3. Add a dispatch arm in `main()` calling `x::x(&args)` (or,
156	   once ported, `x::x(&ctx, &params)`).
157	4. Doc comments on `#[arg(...)]` fields drive `--help`; add
158	   `#[arg(verbatim_doc_comment, …)]` on any field whose doc
159	   comment uses bullets (clap otherwise reflows them into
160	   prose).
161	5. Dynamic value completion (`--account=<TAB>`) is an
162	   `ArgValueCompleter` attached to the leaf in
163	   `src/options_flags/` — completion is a clap-aware concern
164	   and stays at this layer.
165	
166	## args → Context + Params
167	
168	"The Context+Params port" — each subcommand's
169	`pub fn x(args: &XxxArgs)` is structured as
170	`pub fn x(ctx: &Context, params: &XxxParams)`, with an
171	`XxxParams` flat struct + a `From` (or `TryFrom`, if the
172	conversion is fallible) at the binary edge. `init` (0.44.0)
173	is the worked example; complete across all subcommands as of
174	0.49.0, and the shape new subcommands follow. Out of scope
175	until a real consumer surfaces: typed
176	errors, returned-outcomes-vs-`println!`, `ProgressSink`,
177	`Context` fields beyond `UserConfig` + `--log`. Per-subcommand
178	status: `notes/chores/chores-*.md` (the "Refactor tracking" section).
179	
180	## per-subcommand flags → `src/options_flags/`
181	
182	Independently of the Context+Params port — "the options_flags
183	extraction" — per-subcommand inline `#[arg]` fields are being
184	lifted into reusable leaves/bundles under `src/options_flags/`
185	so a flag is defined (parser, help, completer) exactly once. See
186	[`src/options_flags/README.md`](src/options_flags/README.md)
187	for the leaf / bundle / Pattern-A mechanics and the
188	Flag-vs-Option classification; `notes/chores/chores-*.md` for
189	per-subcommand status.
190	
191	## See also
192	
193	- [`README.md`](README.md) — user-facing overview and
194	  per-subcommand usage.
195	- [`CLAUDE.md`](CLAUDE.md) — bot workflow, versioning,
196	  commit/push conventions, code conventions.
197	- [`src/options_flags/README.md`](src/options_flags/README.md)
198	  — leaf/bundle patterns for the options_flags extraction.
199	- [`notes/todo.md`](notes/todo.md) — live task list, including
200	  both refactors.
201	- `notes/chores/chores-*.md` — per-cycle design captures and the
202	  refactor-tracking tables (currently
203	  [`chores-10.md`](notes/chores/chores-10.md); `chores-09.md` and
204	  earlier are closed).
205	
```

1	# Notes
2	
3	This directory contains various notes and documentation related to the project.
4	Each file is organized by topic for easy reference.
5	
6	By default there are chores-*.md and todo.md. Chores are general notes
7	about tasks and todo.md contains short term tasks and their status.
8	The chores-NN files are numbered in sequence; older ones are closed
9	(chores-09.md and earlier — through the 0.48.x cycles), and the
10	active one ([chores-10.md](chores-10.md), the 0.49.0 cycle onward)
11	also carries the **refactor-tracking** tables (the Context+Params
12	port and the options_flags extraction).
13	
14	App architecture — the CLI-args / `Context`+`Params` split, a
15	generic module map, the subcommand model, and what the
16	Context+Params port and the options_flags extraction *are* (the
17	*live status* is in chores-10.md) — lives at the repo root in
18	[`../ARCHITECTURE.md`](../ARCHITECTURE.md).
19	
20	In the future we I expect we may want to create a "notes"
21	database to better manage the information, TBD.
22	
23	Examples chore file:
24	```
25	# Chores-01.md
26	 
27	General maintenance tasks and considerations for the project see other files for
28	more specific topics. A chore in a chores file provides quick information on the
29	how and why of a particular chore.
30	
31	## Create a binary that lists jj info 
32	
33	This binary should list the changeID, commitID, and description title
34	and using `jj-lib`
35	```
36	
37	## Workflow and conventions
38	
39	Bot-facing workflow, versioning, and code conventions live in
40	[`../CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md). Start there for:
41	
42	- **Versioning during development** — single-step vs multi-step,
43	  `-N` pre-release suffixes, done-marker discipline.
44	- **Code Conventions** — doc comments on every file / fn / method,
45	  `// OK: …` justifications on `unwrap*` calls, ask-on-ambiguity,
46	  stuck detection.
47	- **Commit-Push-Finalize Flow** — two-checkpoint per-step
48	  discipline with hard stop after finalize.
49	
50	## Todo format
51	
52	Todo.md is organized into `## In Progress`, `## Todo`, `## Bugs`,
53	and `## Done` sections; each item is a short description with
54	reference links to more detail.
55	
56	`## Todo` and `## Bugs` entries carry explicit `1.` `2.` …
57	numbers in the source so you can grep, count, and reference
58	them ("let's work on #3"). You don't hand-maintain the
59	numbers — insert, delete, or reorder entries freely, then
60	`vc-x1 fix-todo --no-dry-run` renumbers the list and
61	normalizes continuation-line indent. `vc-x1 fix-todo` alone
62	only previews; `vc-x1 validate-todo` is the read-only check.
63	The `## Done` section keeps `-` bullets — items aren't
64	referenced by number once completed.
65	
66	Reference citations are **double-bracketed** — `[[1]]`, or
67	`[[2]],[[3]]` for several — so the brackets show when rendered
68	(a bare `[1]` renders as just `1`, which doesn't read as a
69	reference). Separate multiple with a comma: `[[2]],[[3]]`, not
70	`[2,3]` (a single ref key — won't resolve) or `[[2]][[3]]`. The
71	`[N]:` definition lines and inline `[text](url)` / `[text](#anchor)`
72	links stay single-bracketed (their brackets already show in
73	source, and the link text reads as a link when rendered).
74	
75	Examples:
76	
77	# Todo
78	- Add new feature X [details](features.md#feature-x)
79	- Fix bug Y [[1]]
80	
81	# Done
82	- Fixed issue Z [[2]],[[3]]
83	
84	[1]: bugs.md#bug-y
85	[2]: issues.md#issue-z
86	[3]: fixes.md#fix-z
87	
88	## Reference numbering
89	
90	Every note file (`todo.md`, `chores-NN.md`, `done.md`) keeps a
91	file-local `# References` section at the bottom. Reference numbers
92	are scoped to that file — `[1]` in `chores-07.md` and `[1]` in
93	`chores-01.md` are independent slots that may point at completely
94	different URLs. New chores files start their numbering at `[1]`.
95	
96	Treat `[N]` like a **footnote**: the number is a local slot, only
97	meaningful within its file's `# References`. So a `[N]` *citation*
98	(bare `[N]`, or doubled `[[N]]` — see below) never reuses another
99	file's number; to cite a target a sibling file references, pick
100	your own next-local slot and define it (the same target may carry
101	a different number in each file). A `[N]` *inside a code span*
102	(`` `[72]` ``) is different — that's a quoted identifier: literal
103	text naming a ref-key (often from another file's namespace), data,
104	not a citation; it needs no definition here. To point at a section
105	of another file from prose, use an inline link with an anchor —
106	`[that section](../chores-07.md#…)` — not a bare number.
107	
108	A `chores-NN.md` `# References` entry is usually a
109	`/notes/<file>.md#anchor` (or `/ARCHITECTURE.md`) path, but may
110	also be a **commit reference** —
111	`[N]: <commit-url-with-12-hex-SHA> "<full-40-hex-SHA>"`, cited by
112	a section's `Commits:` line. See
113	[`../CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md#chores-commit-references) for the
114	why and the exact shape.
115	
116	A file's `# References` can be **re-packed** to a contiguous
117	`[1]..[N]` in first-citation-appearance order — walk the file's
118	prose in document order (`todo.md`: `## Todo` then `## Done`;
119	`chores-NN.md`: top to bottom) and number refs as their first
120	`[[N]]` citation appears. This is a file-local rewrite — only
121	that file's `[[N]]` citations and `[N]:` definitions move; every
122	target and sibling file is untouched. A `[[N]]` inside a `` ` ``
123	code span is a literal token, not a citation, and is left alone.
124	Do it opportunistically (when the namespace has drifted enough to
125	annoy), not on a schedule: `todo.md` fragments fastest (entries
126	land and get pruned every cycle) and is the usual candidate;
127	`chores-NN.md` / `done.md` are append-mostly and only need it
128	after an unusual event (e.g. a bulk retrofit that allocated slots
129	out of document order).
130	
131	## Retiring Done entries
132	
133	`todo.md`'s `## Done` section is a rolling buffer of recently shipped
134	work, not a permanent log. Move entries into `done.md` at two natural
135	beats:
136	
137	- **Closing a ladder** — when the final `X.Y.Z` (no suffix) commit
138	  ships, decide which prior entries are no longer needed for nearby
139	  context and migrate them.
140	- **Opening a new ladder** — at `X.Y.Z-0`, do the same sweep before
141	  bumping the version.
142	
143	Migration mechanics:
144	
145	- Move the bullet itself from `todo.md > ## Done` to
146	  `done.md` (preserving the original ref number).
147	- Copy any references the moved entries cite into
148	  `done.md`'s `# References` section (those refs are file-local,
149	  so coexisting with `todo.md`'s namespace is fine).
150	- Prune any references in `todo.md > # References` no longer cited
151	  by anything in `## In Progress` / `## Todo` / `## Done`. This
152	  frees the numbers for future reuse.
153	
```

I'm acquainted. Here's the picture:

## vc-x1 — what it is

A single Rust binary (`vc-x1 <subcommand>`) that manages a **jj-git dual-repo workspace**: an app repo plus a `.claude` bot-session repo, with commits cross-referencing each other via `ochid:` trailers. This project itself is one such workspace (app at `.`, bot at `.claude`).

## Current state

- **Version 0.55.0**, working copy clean, **no cycle in progress** (`@` is empty, parent is `main` at `95ef198`).
- Last cycle landed `validate-todo` + `fix-todo` — read-only check and renumber/normalize for `## Todo`/`## Bugs` entry numbering (replaced `notes/fix-todo.py`).

## Architecture (two-layer)

- **CLI layer (clap-aware)** — `main.rs` (`Cli`/`Commands` + dispatch), `options_flags/` (reusable `#[derive(Args)]` leaves/bundles), `common.rs`.
- **Subcommand layer (clap-free)** — entrypoint `fn x(ctx: &Context, params: &XxxParams)`; `XxxArgs → XxxParams` conversion at the binary edge. Context+Params port complete as of 0.49.0; SubcommandRunner trait sweep done 0.50.0.
- Subcommands: `chid`, `desc`, `list`, `show`, `validate-desc`, `fix-desc`, `validate-todo`, `fix-todo`, `clone`, `init`, `symlink`, `sync`, `finalize`, `push`.

## Conventions I'll follow (from CLAUDE.md)

- Two jj repos; commit with `-R .` / `-R .claude`, `ochid:` trailers both ways.
- Every plan starts with a version bump (single-step → `X.Y.Z`; multi-step → `X.Y.Z-0…N`).
- `vc-x1 push <bookmark>` for the commit-push-finalize flow; **hard stop after finalize**.
- Per-file review checkpoints; never commit/push without explicit approval.
- Doc comments on every file/fn/method; `// OK: …` on every `unwrap*`.
- Speculation gets a "The bot thinks …" marker.

## What's queued

`notes/todo.md` has **45 Todo items** + **1 Bug**. The top of the list:

1. **Codify the move-into-`## In Progress` workflow** (highest priority) — make `## In Progress` self-describing rather than a bare ladder.
2. Single-field `options_flags` leaves → `value` field convention sweep.
3–4. Investigate `linkme` / `inventory` for subcommand registration.

The open bug: `finalize::surface_previous_failures` is racy (stale markers, concurrent double-print, torn reads, no notify-at-failure).

What would you like to work on?

> The overview from ## Todo list which should **an overview** of the problem and a list **things** to do.

- "which should an overview" — missing verb ("which should *be*").
- "a list things to do" — missing "of" ("a list *of* things").

```
255	characters in place, map remaining spaces to hyphens 1-for-1. Do
256	**not** collapse adjacent whitespace — so `a + b` → `a--b` (spaces
257	on both sides of `+`), but `a: b` → `a-b` (only trailing space on
258	`:`). General markdown reference:
259	[markdownguide.org](https://www.markdownguide.org). GitHub
260	publishes no official spec for auto-generated anchors; the
261	de-facto reference implementation is
262	[github-slugger](https://github.com/Flet/github-slugger).
263	
264	### Versioning
265	
266	Every plan must start with a version bump. Choose the approach based
267	on scope:
268	
269	- **Single-step** (recommended for mechanical/focused changes): bump
270	  directly to `X.Y.Z`, implement in one commit. Simpler history.
271	- **Multi-step** (for exploratory/large changes): bump to `X.Y.Z-0`,
272	  implement across multiple commits incrementing the numeric
273	  suffix. The final commit drops the suffix.
274	
275	The plan should recommend one approach and get user approval before
276	starting.
277	
278	A plan that picks up a `## Todo` item — or *re-scopes* an
279	existing `## In Progress` ladder to absorb one — **deletes that
280	`## Todo` entry in the same commit** (the version-bump / plan
281	commit). `## In Progress` is the sole record of that work until
282	close-out, when it moves to `## Done`; a cycle that merges
283	several entangled `## Todo` items deletes all of them. A `## Todo`
284	entry that duplicates current `## In Progress` work is a process
285	bug.
286	
287	For multi-step:
288	
289	1. Bump version to `X.Y.Z-0` with the plan and commit as a chore
290	   marker.
291	2. Implement in one or more `X.Y.Z-N` commits (increment N as
292	   needed).
293	3. Final commit bumps to `X.Y.Z` (no suffix), updates
294	   `notes/todo.md` and `notes/chores/chores-*.md` — this is the "done"
295	   marker.
296	
297	Multi-step cycles surface the ladder at the top of
298	`notes/todo.md > ## In Progress` as a bullet list with `(done)` /
299	`(current)` markers — see the file's intro paragraph for the
300	format. When starting a new step, the *first* edit is to mark
301	that step `(current)` in `notes/todo.md` — before any code/doc
302	work — so the In Progress view reflects what's actually
303	happening. The flip back to `(done)` is part of the pre-commit
304	checklist (item 6).
305	
306	**Why numeric suffixes (`-0`, `-1`, …) rather than `-devN`:**
307	semver pre-release identifiers may consist of a single numeric
308	component, and they compare numerically per spec. So
309	`X.Y.Z-1 < X.Y.Z-2 < … < X.Y.Z` correctly orders the dev ladder
310	below the done marker. Cargo accepts this form. The `-dev` prefix
311	adds no information the git log doesn't already convey and
312	doubles typing per commit.
313	
314	The final release commit (no suffix) signals completion rather than
315	amending prior commits. This keeps history readable and makes it easy
316	to see which commits were exploratory vs final.
317	
318	For decomposition finer than `X.Y.Z-N` — sub-steps (`X.Y.Z-N.M`)
319	or sub-sub-steps (`X.Y.Z-N.M-K`) — see
320	[Sub-step Workflow](#sub-step-workflow). The cycle close-out
321	choice (squash to one commit vs land each sub-step separately)
322	is made at close-out, not cycle start.
323	
324	See also [`notes/substep-protocol.md`](notes/substep-protocol.md)
325	for the full protocol — when to use, per-substep contract
326	(`cargo test --bins` is non-negotiable), navigation, the validated
327	close-out squash recipe, and recovery. Revset primitives the
328	protocol relies on (`@`, `@-`, `..`, `::`, prefix matching) live in
329	[`notes/jj-revsets.md`](notes/jj-revsets.md).
```

For a **single-step** cycle, "pickup" and "close-out" both happen before the one commit. So I'll *populate* `## In Progress` with #1's text as the first edit, you review the working copy (the `## In Progress` block included) during the code loop, then I *clear* it and write the `## Done` one-liner at close-out. The single commit's diff shows `## In Progress` unchanged (`_No cycle currently in progress._` → same) — the populated state lived only in the working copy you reviewed. That's expected and fine; multi-step is where `## In Progress` actually persists into intermediate commits.

## Proposed plan — single-step, `docs:`, version **0.56.0**

**Cargo.toml** — `0.55.0` → `0.56.0`.

- `vc-x1 fix-todo --no-dry-run` to renumber the remaining entries.
- At close-out: clear `## In Progress` back to `_No cycle currently in progress._`, add a `## Done` one-liner + `[N]` ref.

**CLAUDE.md `### Versioning` sweep**

- Pre-commit checklist item (b) — "delete that `## Todo` entry" → "move it into `## In Progress`".

> DEVELOPER

That looks good, but here's a thought, we use 3 jj nodes to do the 3 "sub-steps" 1) Update chores + In Progress 2) do the work 3) close-out. With reviews at each sub-step and then after the "close-out" we squash so there is only a single commit. Thoughts?

> AGENT

I like it — and it actually fits your earlier "In Progress info at review time" point *better* than the single-step version.

## Why it's better

With single-step, the populated `## In Progress` lived only in transient working-copy state. With 3 nodes:

- **Node 1** commit has `## In Progress` populated → it's a real (mutable, pre-squash) committed revision you review in your editor with full file context.
- **Node 2** still has it populated while the CLAUDE.md rewrite lands.
- **Node 3** clears it + adds the `## Done` line.
- The squash erases `## In Progress` churn from the final `0.56.0` commit on `main` — exactly your intent.

So the In-Progress content is reviewable as committed history *during* the cycle, then gone from permanent history. Cleaner story.

## It's already CLAUDE.md machinery

This is the **Sub-step Workflow's commit-first review model** (commit each node, you review the committed revision, I iterate via `jj squash --into @-`) plus the **squash close-out** option — both already documented. Nothing new to invent; we're just applying it to a 3-node cycle.

## Two things to settle

**Numbering** — I'd version the nodes `0.56.0-0`, `0.56.0-1`, `0.56.0-2` (Cargo.toml + commit titles per the sub-step rule), then squash to `0.56.0`. Node 3 / the squash is where Cargo.toml drops the suffix.

**The one real cost** — CLAUDE.md mandates the full cargo cycle (`fmt`/`clippy`/`test` + `cargo install --locked`) *per sub-step*. This cycle is docs-only — nothing compiles differently — so `cargo install` 3× is pure waste. I'd suggest: skip `cargo install` on nodes 1–2, run the full cycle once at close-out (node 3). `cargo fmt`/`test` are cheap enough to run per node if you want the habit. Your call.

Also: each node pairs an app commit with a `.claude` commit (ochid trailers both ways) — 3 pairs — and `.claude` keeps its per-node commits as the journal (only the app side squashes).

One honest caveat: for a ~3-paragraph docs change this is a fair bit of ceremony. It's worth it if you want to dogfood the sub-step workflow and review committed revisions; a single-step with per-file checkpoints would also do the job. You've clearly thought about it and I think it's a sound choice — just naming the tradeoff.

Confirm the `0.56.0-0/-1/-2` numbering and the cargo-cycle call, and I'll start with node 1 (chores opener + populate `## In Progress` + version bump).

> DEVELOPER

You're correct for this trivial change this is to much ceremony, but as a dogfooding excercise I think it's worth it. In particular, should we create a description for each of these sub-steps, I hadn't considered that as I wasn't contemplating doing pushes to github, but adding a description doesn't mean pushing. But I'd would like to use zed to review the changes and that may mean we need a description, I'm not sure. Thoughts?

> AGENT

Yes — each sub-step gets a description, and you've got the key fact right: **a description is local-only.** `jj describe` / `jj commit -m` writes commit metadata in your local repo; `jj git push` is the *only* thing that touches GitHub, and in this plan it happens exactly once, at close-out, on the squashed commit.

A few points:

**It's not really optional under the protocol.** The commit-first review model has you `jj commit` each sub-step (not `jj new` undescribed nodes), and the ochid trailers live *in the description*. So a description per node is already required by the sub-step workflow — describing them isn't extra ceremony you're adding, it's the workflow as written.

**Zed angle.** Zed doesn't need a description to *render* a diff — a diff is a diff. But it needs one for you to *pick the right commit*. Three nodes all showing `(no description set)` in the log / commit graph are indistinguishable; with titles you can tell node 1 from node 3 at a glance. Practical setup:

- Configure jj's diff tool once in `~/.config/jj/config.toml`: `diff-editor = ["zed", "--diff", "$left", "$right"]`.
- Review each node with `jj diff -r @-` (the node I just committed — `jj commit` leaves `@` empty on top) or `jj diff -r <change-id>` for an earlier one.
- Descriptions make `jj log` readable so you know which `-r` to pass.

**Efficiency suggestion.** Since the squash discards the per-node descriptions, don't triplicate the polished file-by-file body. Per node: a real title (`… (0.56.0-N)`) + a short body (1–3 bullets of what that node did) + the ochid trailer. The full file-by-file body gets composed *once*, for the squashed `0.56.0` commit, at the close-out `vc-x1 push main` message step. Per-node descriptions stay useful for navigation without you writing three throwaway essays.

So the flow is: per node — edit → `jj commit -m … -R .` + paired `.claude` commit → you review in Zed → iterate via `jj squash --into @-` if needed. Close-out — squash the 3 app nodes, `jj describe` the result with the final message, one `vc-x1 push main`.

Confirmed numbering `0.56.0-0/-1/-2` → squash `0.56.0`, full cargo cycle once at node 3. Ready to start node 1 (chores opener + populate `## In Progress` + version bump) on your go.

> DEVELOPER

The "bot" repo is a linear history and we should "minimize/never" squash of it. Hence I'm thinking we shouldn't do the duplicate "title/body" ceremony as we do with a "multi-step", thoughts?

> AGENT

Agree. The "same title/body in both repos" rule pays off only when both repos keep parallel commits — normal multi-step, no squash. Here the app side squashes 3 → 1 while `.claude` stays linear, so copying the three app messages onto `.claude` would permanently store three `.claude` commits echoing app messages that no longer exist on the app side. Pointless duplication.

It also lines up with a rule CLAUDE.md *already* has, that `vc-x1 push` quietly overrides: **"Session-repo body: bullets describe in-session activity rather than code changes."** Push copies the app message because it composes one message for both commits — but the per-sub-step `.claude` commits here are plain `jj commit`s, not push, so we can follow the session-activity rule as written.

**Concrete shape:**
- App nodes `0.56.0-0/-1/-2` — real title + 1–3 bullet body, but throwaway (squashed at close-out). The polished file-by-file body is composed once for the squashed `0.56.0`, at the close-out push.
- `.claude` nodes — their own lightweight session-journal messages (what the session did that node). No app-message copying. Kept, not squashed.

**The one consequence to handle — ochid.** ochid pairs commits by change ID, and the app squash collapses 3 app change IDs into 1. The clean fix needs no fixup: we squash the app stack *downward into node1* (the natural direction), so **node1's change ID survives as the final `0.56.0` commit**. Therefore — point every `.claude` node's `ochid` at **node1's app change ID** from the start. node1's ID exists the moment we commit node1, so all three `.claude` commits can reference it, and after the squash they're all already correct. The app `0.56.0` commit just gets its ochid (→ the final `.claude` commit) set fresh when we compose the close-out message — normal close-out work.

So: zero ochid fixup, at the cost of committing upfront to "squash into node1" — which is the obvious direction anyway, and we've already decided to squash.

This is good dogfooding signal: CLAUDE.md doesn't currently address sub-step-squash crossed with the linear bot repo. I'll capture it in this cycle's chores section, and it may deserve a follow-up todo to codify (the squash-into-node1 ochid convention, the `.claude` session-message exemption).

Ready to start node 1 on your go.

> DEVELOPER

Hmm, I think there is a misunderstanding, on these sub-steps there will be only
one jj node on the "bot" repo and thus no description is needed until the close out.
On the "code" side all three nodes can point to the @ node on the "bot" side.
I definitely might be wrong, but that's what I think.

> AGENT

You're right — and it's cleaner than what I described. I over-applied CLAUDE.md's per-sub-step `.claude` cadence. Let me concede that and check the mechanics, since the whole thing rests on one jj fact.

**The load-bearing fact: jj change IDs are stable across rewrites.** A change ID is assigned once and survives working-copy snapshots (editing files in `@`), `jj describe`, `jj squash`, and rebases. It's only replaced by `jj commit` (which creates a *new* empty `@` — but the finalized commit *keeps* the old `@`'s change ID) or `jj new`.

So your model holds:

- The `.claude` `@` has a change ID **right now**, stable as long as we don't `jj commit -R .claude` or `jj new -R .claude`. Session data accumulating into it = snapshots = ID unchanged. `jj describe` at close-out = ID unchanged. Push's finalize `jj commit` = the finalized `.claude` commit *keeps* that ID.
- Therefore all three app sub-step commits carry the **same** `ochid: /.claude/<.claude-@-id>`, and it stays valid forever — through close-out and beyond. No N:1 mess, no fixup. One `.claude` node, one description, written once at close-out.

**Bonus: both directions are deterministic from cycle start.** App node1 = the current app `@` `ynunuxxo`. We squash node2/node3 *downward into node1*, so the final `0.56.0` commit's change ID is also `ynunuxxo`. So `.claude`'s close-out ochid → `/ynunuxxo` is known now too. Zero fixup either direction.

**This does contradict CLAUDE.md's `.claude` cadence section** — which explicitly *rejected* "accumulate across the cycle, commit once at close-out." But the rejection's rationale was "preserves flexibility — any sub-step can be promoted to its own push." A **squash** close-out voids that: no sub-step gets promoted. So the honest rule is:

- **keep-separate** close-out → per-sub-step `.claude` commits (current rule).
- **squash** close-out → one `.claude` node, committed once. Your model.

The only thing given up is sub-step-granular session snapshots — irrelevant for a 3-node docs cycle.

So, practically: capture the `.claude` `@` change ID once at node-1 start (`vc-x1 chid -s bot -L`); all three app ochids use it; `.claude` gets its single description at close-out. And this is solid dogfooding signal — I'll note the cadence carve-out in the chores section and it likely deserves a follow-up todo to codify in CLAUDE.md.

```
855	column-0 lines are dropped from the measurement and passed
856	through untouched. Flagging them as malformed input is a
857	separate follow-up captured in `## Todo`.
858	
859	## feat: validate-todo + fix-todo (0.55.0)
860	
861	Cycle close-out. The `validate-todo` / `fix-todo` subcommand
862	pair — a Rust replacement for `notes/fix-todo.py` — is in:
863	`validate-todo` checks `## Todo` / `## Bugs` numbering and
864	continuation indent, `fix-todo` renumbers and re-indents
865	(dry-run by default). Both share the `todo_helpers` core and
866	print an identical per-entry report line.
867	
868	### As-built ladder
869	
870	- 0.55.0-0 plan + version bump + chores opener
871	- 0.55.0-1 add validate-todo subcommand + todo_helpers
872	- 0.55.0-2 add fix-todo subcommand; docs sweep; delete
873	  notes/fix-todo.py
874	- 0.55.0 close-out
875	
876	### Outcome
877	
878	Two subcommands and a docs sweep over three steps, no
879	evaluation-gate detours — the subcommand work landed as
880	planned.
881	
882	The one reversal was the opener's workflow experiment.
883	`0.55.0-0`'s `### Keeping the active task in ## Todo`
884	switched CLAUDE.md from delete-on-pickup to a keep-#1 model
885	(the picked-up `## Todo` entry stays under a `### Current In
886	Progress` heading, linked from `## In Progress`). It did its
887	job — the active task stayed visible in `## Todo` for easy
888	dogfooding mid-cycle — but at close-out the user judged it
889	not worth keeping. This commit reverts CLAUDE.md to plain
890	delete-on-pickup. A refined idea — move the picked-up entry
891	*into* `## In Progress` (self-describing, no heading hack)
892	and capture it in the chores opener — is queued as a
893	`## Todo` item for a later cycle.
894	
895	Dropping `## Todo` item 1 and renumbering was done with
896	`vc-x1 fix-todo --no-dry-run` — the cycle's own tool on its
897	own backlog, and the close-out dogfood.
898
```

1) Prepare and Plan, commit + desc
2) Do work, commit + desc
3) Prepare close-out, commit, squash 1..3, desc + push

A multi step is:
1) Prepare and Plan, commit + desc + push
2) Do work, commit + desc + push, loop to 2)
3) Prepare close-out, commit + desc + push

So they are verify similar, other than in single step missing pushes in 1 & 2 and step 3 includes a squash, is that simpler?

> AGENT

You're right — structurally they're the same protocol. Let me sharpen *why*, because I think it points at a real cleanup.

## The two are the same 3-phase protocol

Plan → Work(×N) → Close-out. What actually differs is **two independent settings**, and the current taxonomy bundles them into one label:

- **Decomposition** — how many local Work commits: 1, or N.
- **Remote shape** — what lands on `origin`: every commit pushed as it's made (N remote commits), or held and squashed to 1, pushed once.

"Single-step" and "multi-step" each pick *both* settings at once, as a bundle. That's why "single-step with 3 sub-steps" sounds self-contradictory — it is, under the current vocabulary. What we're actually running is a **multi-step ladder (`0.56.0-0/-1/-2`) with pushes withheld and a squash close-out**. Calling it "single-step" only describes the *outcome* (one commit on `main`), not the work.

## Is it simpler?

Honest answer: it gives a simpler **remote history** (one commit per logical change, no half-finished dev markers) and simpler **review** (no mid-cycle remote state to reason about) — at the cost of the squash plus the `.claude`-cadence / ochid bookkeeping we had to work out. It is *not* simpler *process*; it's the same work, differently optimized. For a trivial cycle it's pure dogfooding cost, which you already accepted.

## The concrete consequence for what you're reviewing

This is exactly why the last paragraph of my CLAUDE.md rewrite reads awkwardly:

> "For a single-step or squash close-out cycle, pickup and close-out both land before the one commit..."

That paragraph *entangles* the pickup rule with push/squash policy — the very entanglement you just spotted. **I recommend dropping it entirely.** The `**Pickup:**` bullet already says "the chores opener **mirrors** that content so the cycle stays readable once `## In Progress` is cleared" — that states the durable-capture mechanism *uniformly*, for every cycle shape. Whether `## In Progress` churn itself survives in pushed history is a push/squash concern, not part of the pickup rule, and doesn't need saying.

So the In-Progress rule stays uniform: pickup populates, close-out clears, chores opener mirrors. No cycle-type carve-out.

## The bigger idea

Re-basing CLAUDE.md's taxonomy onto those two axes (decomposition × remote-shape) instead of single/multi/sub-step is genuinely worth doing — but it's a separate cycle, not this one (this cycle = In-Progress codification). I'd capture it as a `## Todo` item.

How do you want to proceed — drop that paragraph and finish node 2 as planned, and add a todo for the taxonomy rework?

> AGENT

Yes, makes sense — the nomenclature is good. Let me lock the model so we're aligned before I touch CLAUDE.md:

**The cycle protocol — three phases:**
1. **Preparation** — version bump; pickup (`## Todo` item → `## In Progress`); chores opener (mirrors `## In Progress`).
2. **Work-N** — the implementation; 1..N commits.
3. **Close-out** — `## In Progress` item → `## Done` one-liner; finalize the chores section.

**Two modes — how the cycle reaches the remote:**
- **multi-step** — commit + push at the end of Preparation, every Work commit, and Close-out. N+2 commits on `origin`.
- **single-step** — Preparation and Work commits stay local; Close-out squashes the N+2 stack into one and pushes once.

Preparation and Close-out bracket the cycle, so "the work" is exactly Work-N between them — your identifiability point. (One naming snag: "single-step" now produces one commit via *squash*, not by being one step. `Squashed` / `Incremental` describe what actually differs. Your call — I'll note it, not insist.)

**This rescopes our cycle** from "codify In-Progress pickup" to "refine the cycle protocol" — In-Progress pickup becomes one part. That's a legitimate `## In Progress` re-scope (the rule we're writing literally allows it). I'll update `## In Progress`, the chores opener, and the cycle title to the broader scope as part of the work.

**The one decision I need before editing** — the existing `## Sub-step Workflow` section (~200 lines). A flat **Work-N** makes the sub-step / sub-sub-step *version nesting* (`X.Y.Z-N.M`, `X.Y.Z-N.M-K`) redundant: "more granularity" just means "more Work commits." Two ways:

- **(a) Flatten** — collapse single/multi/sub/sub-sub into the one Prep/Work-N/Close-out protocol + two modes; delete the version-nesting; re-home the still-good mechanical content (commit-first review model, per-commit cargo cycle, ochid trailers, reviewing committed revisions) under it. Big clarity win, rewrites a large section.
- **(b) Keep the nesting** — bolt the phase names on top, leave `X.Y.Z-N.M` as-is. Less churn, redundancy stays.

I recommend **(a)** — it's the actual simplification you're after — but it's a big rewrite, so I want your call first.

**Ladder** stays `0.56.0-0/-1/-2` → squash `0.56.0`: node 2 (`0.56.0-1`, the current working copy) becomes the Work commit that restructures `### Versioning` + `## Sub-step Workflow`; node 3 stays Close-out.

> AGENT

Good refinements. Going through them:

**single-push / multi-push** — agree. "Push" is the right axis word, and it kills the "single-step that's actually ≥3 steps" contradiction.

**Keep nesting** — agreed, dropping my flatten idea. Subdividing a Work item that turns out bigger than planned is a real need, and nesting handles it without renumbering siblings.

**single-push without squash** — your read holds together. The clean way to state it is two axes:
- *push timing*: incremental (multi-push) vs once (single-push)
- *squash*: yes / no

`.claude` commits once per push (it captures session state at the push point), so:
- **multi-push** → per-phase `.claude` commits, paired 1:1 with code commits, one ochid each.
- **single-push** → one `.claude` node for the whole cycle.
  - *+squash*: code collapses to one commit → 1:1.
  - *no squash*: code keeps N+2 commits → N:1; the `.claude` description carries a **list** of ochids, every code commit carries the same single `.claude` ochid. Exactly as you said.

So single-push-no-squash has multi-push's remote shape but single-push's `.claude` structure — a coherent corner, worth documenting, probably not the common case.

**Numbering — one push-back, one honesty note.**

*Push-back: drop the dot-vs-dash split.* Your examples use `.` for a simple subdivision (`-3.1`) and `-` for a complex one (`-3-0`, `-3-1`) — same concept, a nested work item, with two notations chosen by whether the level has a Preparation. That's fragile: a simple subdivision that later needs a Preparation forces a `-3.1 → -3-1` renumber.

Use **one separator, `.`**, everywhere (semver mandates `-` once at the start of the prerelease, then `.` for every level after). Rule generalizes yours: **a trailing `0` identifier marks a Preparation** — at any depth:

```
x.y.z-0       Preparation (top)
x.y.z-1..n    Work (top)
x.y.z         Close-out (top)

x.y.z-3.0     Preparation of the -3 sub-cycle
x.y.z-3.1..n  Work
x.y.z-3       Close-out of -3

x.y.z-3.1.0   Preparation of the -3.1 sub-sub-cycle ...
```

A simple subdivision is just `-3.1, -3.2, -3`. If it later needs a Preparation, add `-3.0` — siblings untouched. One rule, nests to any depth. (This changes the existing sub-sub notation `-3.4-1` → `-3.4.1`; going-forward only, past commits keep their numbers.)

*Honesty note on ordering.* A **nested close-out sorts before its children**: semver-wise `x.y.z-3 < x.y.z-3.0 < x.y.z-3.1` — a prefix always precedes its extensions. So within a nested sub-cycle the version order isn't chronological. It's cosmetic (nothing depends on it; the one real guarantee — bare `X.Y.Z` outranks every dev version — still holds). But note: CLAUDE.md *currently claims* `0.6.0-3.4-1 < 0.6.0-3.4-2 < 0.6.0-3.4`, and that tail is already false — the close-out `-3.4` sorts *first*. The rewrite should correct it: "nested close-outs sort before their children; the only ordering guarantee is final `X.Y.Z` > all dev versions."

Confirm the consistent-`.` scheme and I'll fold all of this into the CLAUDE.md rewrite.

1	# Forking and multi-user coordination
2	
3	Captures design discussion about two related but separable
4	concerns:
5	
6	1. **Forking** a dual-repo workspace — working on an older
7	   code-side base while keeping the bot side append-only.
8	2. **Multi-user collaboration** on the same dual-repo —
9	   multiple humans, multiple bot sessions, code-side merges.
10	
11	Both surface the same underlying tension: the code repo has
12	the classic git/jj DAG (branches, merges, rebases), while the
13	bot repo is fundamentally an append-only journal of
14	conversations that should never be rewritten.
15	
16	For the *content* of the bot repo (vendor identity, format
17	versioning, multi-bot conventions, vendor-subdir directory
18	layout), see the companion file
19	[`bot-data-formats.md`](bot-data-formats.md).
20	
21	This file is forward-looking: most of what it describes is
22	the bot thinks, not implemented. Treat as a design reference,
23	not a status doc.
24	
25	## Forking with the bot side staying linear
26	
27	### The asymmetric situation
28	
29	When work needs to happen on an older code-side base — e.g.,
30	0.41.1 work branched off 0.41.0 while 0.42.0 is in flight on
31	main — the code repo can fork without difficulty. That's a
32	normal git/jj branch.
33	
34	The bot repo can't fork the same way: each session is a linear
35	conversation, and rewriting an existing session line would
36	falsify the historical record. The conversation that happened
37	is the conversation that happened; it doesn't get re-played
38	from a different branch base.
39	
40	### chid stability across rebase
41	
42	jj's two-id model is built for this scenario. Each commit has
43	both:
44	
45	- `commit_id` — git SHA, changes on rewrite.
46	- `change_id` — stable across `describe` / `rebase` /
47	  `squash-into-self`.
48	
49	`ochid:` trailers carry change_ids. So when a code-side branch
50	later rebases on top of another branch, change_ids are
51	preserved and ochid trailers in both directions still resolve.
52	
53	The bot thinks the only operations that break this are
54	`jj squash` (folding two commits into one — collapses two
55	change_ids into one) and `jj abandon` (removing a commit —
56	loses the change_id entirely). Pure rebase is safe.
57	
58	### Why not time-based linkers
59	
60	Time isn't a stronger primitive than change_id:
61	
62	- Clock skew between processes / repos breaks ordering.
63	- Time still doesn't survive rewrites any better than
64	  change_id does — you'd still need a stable identifier baked
65	  into the trailer that matches some property of the
66	  counterpart commit.
67	- change_id is jj's intended stability primitive — that's
68	  literally the job it exists to do.
69	
70	If the change_id scheme ever shows weakness, the bot thinks
71	the answer is jj-side hygiene (don't `squash`-collapse during
72	rebase) rather than switching primitives.
73	
74	### Partner bookmarks
75	
76	The chosen technique for forking the code side while keeping
77	the bot side linear: **partner bookmarks**.
78	
79	When a code-side branch needs to advance independently, create
80	a bookmark of the same name on the bot side starting at the
81	*current* bot HEAD — not at the historical bot commit paired
82	with the code-side branch base. The two bookmarks share a name
83	and advance together via `vc-x1 push`, but each carries its
84	own repo's state.
85	
86	Other names considered and rejected:
87	
88	- **mirror** — implies symmetric content. The bookmarks are
89	  not mirrors; they hold different content with no
90	  point-by-point correspondence.
91	- **tandem** — captures cooperation, but slightly less common
92	  in software vocabulary.
93	- **paired** — bland; "pair" can imply matching halves.
94	- **disjoint** / **asymmetric** — describe a property of the
95	  relationship, not a label for the technique.
96	- **yoked** — accurate metaphor but obscure.
97	
98	**partner** won because it conveys cooperation without
99	implying duplication. Always subject to change as we live
100	with the term.
101	
102	The asymmetric ordering between code and bot history (rebased
103	main commits on the code side will eventually point at bot
104	commits that come *before* their partner-branch pairs in bot
105	history) is a cosmetic wrinkle, not a correctness problem —
106	change_ids resolve regardless of position.
107	
108	### Recovery anchor
109	
110	The original main HEAD — the commit being "branched away
111	from" — should stay put, not be rewritten or rewound. It's
112	the recovery anchor for the operation. If the partner-branch
113	work goes wrong, main is still where it was, and recovery is
114	`jj abandon` on the new branch's commits.
115	
116	In the 0.41.1 init+clone refactor, this means main stays at
117	its current 0.42.0-4 docs-capture commit (`55eadc8`) for the
118	duration of the side-branch work. That commit becomes
119	redundant after the eventual rebase — its content has been
120	re-landed on `init-clone-refactor` — and gets dropped during
121	the rebase rather than being rewound now.
122	
123	## Multi-user collaboration
124	
125	The bot thinks the same model — linear forest on the bot
126	side, multi-trailer reconciliation on the code side —
127	generalizes naturally to multi-user.
128	
129	### Bot repo as a forest of linear chains
130	
131	Each session is one append-only chain. The bot repo is a
132	forest of those chains; never merged. No bot-side merge
133	commits at all. Code-side has a DAG (merges, branches);
134	bot-side is a forest. The mapping is the multi-ochid trailer
135	set on each code commit, fanning out to the contributing
136	chain tips.
137	
138	The bot thinks this is cleaner than "bot side mirrors code
139	side topology" because:
140	
141	- Each conversation is a wire-format record of what was
142	  actually said. Merging two conversations into one commit
143	  would falsify that record.
144	- "Never rewrite bot commits" becomes an enforceable rule —
145	  change_ids on the bot side are automatically stable.
146	- Storage and sync stay simple — appending only, no merge
147	  conflict resolution on the bot side ever.
148	
149	### Multi-line ochid trailer
150	
151	Standard git trailer convention allows multiple values for
152	the same key. Just emit `ochid:` once per pointer:
153	
154	```
155	ochid: /.claude/abc123
156	ochid: /.claude/def456
157	ochid: /.claude/ghi789
158	```
159	
160	Single-pointer commits stay backward compatible — they're
161	just the N=1 case. No version bump needed; existing parsers
162	either already accept repeated trailers or are easy to
163	extend.
164	
165	### When multi-ochid actually applies
166	
167	- **Code-side merge with no extra bot work** — N ochids =
168	  the merge parents' contributing tip change_ids (one per
169	  parent). Possibly with de-duplication if parents share
170	  ancestry.
171	- **Code-side merge with conflict-resolution session** —
172	  N parents' ochids + the resolution session's tip ochid.
173	  If user A and user B merge into M and user C resolves the
174	  conflict, M has 3 ochids: A's tip, B's tip, C's session
175	  tip.
176	- **Single-user single-session** — still 1 ochid. Same as
177	  today.
178	- **Code rebase** — ochids unchanged (change_ids are
179	  stable). Same as today. Multi-user rebases work the same
180	  as single-user.
181	
182	### Per-user vs shared bot repo
183	
184	Open question worth deciding before code lands.
185	
186	- **Shared bot repo** (today's model extended): one
187	  `.claude/` repo holds all sessions across all users. Push
188	  from any user appends a chain. Pros: viewer sees
189	  everything in one place; cross-references resolve locally.
190	  Cons: every user pulls every other user's session data on
191	  sync — could be heavy. Permissions are coarse.
192	- **Per-user bot repos**: each user has their own
193	  `.claude-<user>/` repo, ochid trailers carry a user
194	  qualifier (`ochid: /.claude-alice/abc123`). Pros:
195	  lightweight sync, natural permissions. Cons: viewer needs
196	  to know all the bot-repo URLs to show full context;
197	  offline viewing breaks if you don't have the relevant
198	  user's repo.
199	
200	The bot thinks **shared** is the default with a per-project
201	escape hatch — most teams want the cross-references to
202	"just work." The qualifier in ochid (the path component
203	after the leading `/`) already encodes either model:
204	`/.claude/<chid>` for shared, `/.claude-alice/<chid>` for
205	per-user. The trailer format itself is forward-compatible.
206	
207	### Per-user bot repos via URL-shaped ochid
208	
209	Per-user bot repos become practical for distributed
210	projects (open-source, multi-org) when ochid trailers can
211	qualify by URL rather than only workspace-relative path.
212	This generalizes the per-user model in the section above
213	from "co-located teams sharing a workspace" to "anyone on
214	the public internet."
215	
216	**Trailer shape.** Existing path form is
217	workspace-root-relative (`/.claude/<chid>` for shared,
218	`/.claude-alice/<chid>` for per-user-in-shared-workspace).
219	URL form generalizes the qualifier to a remote repo:
220	
221	```
222	ochid: https://github.com/alice/bot-sessions#<chid>
223	ochid: git@github.com:alice/bot-sessions.git#<chid>
224	```
225	
226	Parser dispatch is one rule: if the qualifier parses as a
227	URL, fetch over the network; otherwise resolve relative
228	to workspace root. Existing path-form trailers stay the
229	N=1 backward-compatible case. No schema versioning
230	needed.
231	
232	**Link rot is the load-bearing problem.** A contributor
233	can publish their bot repo, get merged, then later make
234	it private or delete it. The ochid URL 404s; bot context
235	is lost. The bot thinks this is a real-but-not-
236	catastrophic leak — the commit's code and
237	`Signed-off-by:` line remain authoritative; bot context
238	is a nice-to-have for understanding patch development,
239	not a load-bearing semantic. Direct analogue: dead URLs
240	in academic citations, mailing-list links in older
241	kernel commit messages. Annoying for archaeology, not
242	fatal.
243	
244	**Mitigations, in order of practical adoption:**
245	
246	1. **Project-side mirroring at merge time.** When a
247	   maintainer accepts a PR, project tooling fetches the
248	   contributor's bot repo at the referenced chid and
249	   mirrors to a project-namespace archive (e.g.
250	   `bot-archives/<contributor>-<date>-<chid>.jsonl`).
251	   Future ochid resolution falls back to the mirror if
252	   the original URL 404s. The bot thinks this is the
253	   only mitigation most projects would actually adopt —
254	   cheap, obvious upside, no friction for contributors.
255	   Same pattern as LKML's permanent archive.
256	2. **Cryptographic stapling.** Embed a hash of the bot
257	   repo's tip at submission time:
258	   `ochid: <url>#<chid>;sha256=<hash>`. Tamper-detection
259	   stays sound even if retrieval fails; doesn't recover
260	   data. Adds maintenance burden to trailer parsing.
261	3. **CI-enforced live ochid at merge.** Project CI
262	   fetches the URL, verifies it resolves with parseable
263	   content, before allowing merge. Doesn't prevent
264	   later lock/delete but ensures the merge moment was
265	   sound. Contentious in open-source culture (gates
266	   contributors on infra they don't own).
267	
268	**Opt-out via project policy.** A no-bot-context commit
269	isn't a leak — it's a project policy choice. A project
270	that wants bot transparency mandates each commit carry
271	≥1 `ochid:` trailer or an explicit
272	`No-bot-context: <reason>` trailer. Enforceable in CI,
273	identical pattern to existing `Signed-off-by:`.
274	Contributors who want privacy decline that project's
275	contribution norms; their commits go elsewhere or omit
276	the trailer where the project allows.
277	
278	**Cross-bot interop bonus.** Once URL-shaped, the
279	trailer doesn't have to point at a Claude Code repo.
280	Codex, Cursor, Aider, custom in-house tools — anyone
281	whose bot leaves a publishable session record can host
282	their own bot repo and emit ochid trailers in the same
283	format. The trailer becomes "where to find the AI
284	session that produced this commit," not "where to find
285	the Claude Code session." Strictly bigger ecosystem
286	play with no extra design cost.
287	
288	**Implication for shared-central scaling.** URL-shaped
289	per-user repos potentially short-circuit the
290	size/threshold problem documented below. Each
291	contributor pays for their own bot repo; project pays
292	for an opt-in mirror archive sized per its policy. The
293	"50 devs share one ~15G bot repo" problem becomes "50
294	devs each maintain their own ~300M bot repo, project
295	mirrors selectively."
296	
297	Trade-off: shared-central keeps cross-references local
298	and fast; per-user-via-URL needs a network fetch (or a
299	warm mirror) to resolve any non-local trailer. Pick by
300	project shape — small co-located teams favor shared;
301	distributed open-source favors per-user-via-URL.
302	
303	### Bot-side commit immutability
304	
305	Codify "never rewrite bot commits" as a hard rule. The
306	reason: change_ids on the bot side need to be permanently
307	stable for ochid trailers from the code side to resolve
308	forever. Even `jj describe` (description-only) changes the
309	commit_id, which doesn't break change_ids but does signal
310	that bot commits are mutable in a way they shouldn't be.
311	
312	Trade: any commit-level metadata fix has to be done by
313	appending a new commit, not amending the existing one.
314	Awkward but correct.
315	
316	### Bot-repo size and scaling thresholds
317	
318	Concrete measurement on a single-developer vc-x1 project
319	(2026-05-07, after `cargo clean`):
320	
321	- Code repo (excluding `.claude`): ~4M
322	- Bot repo (`.claude`): 311M
323	- Ratio: ~78:1 bot-to-code
324	
325	Cause: every Claude Code session writes a UUID-named
326	`.jsonl` file. Content is mostly unique per session —
327	git/jj packs each file's append history well, but
328	cross-file dedup is weak. So bot-repo bytes scale roughly
329	linearly with total session count.
330	
331	Linear extrapolation to shared-central multi-user (the bot
332	thinks this is a reasonable upper bound; actual savings
333	from cross-session dedup are small):
334	
335	- 5 devs: ~1.5G
336	- 20 devs: ~6G
337	- 50 devs: ~15G
338	
339	Rough operational thresholds:
340	
341	- **≤ ~10 users:** shared-central is fine.
342	- **~10-50 users:** shared-central starts hurting
343	  (multi-minute initial clone; noticeable `vc-x1 sync`
344	  pulls).
345	- **> ~50 users or > ~10G bot repo:** mitigations become
346	  necessary.
347	
348	### Monotonic-growth asymmetry
349	
350	Bot-repo size only grows. Once a session jsonl is
351	committed, the bot-side immutability rule keeps it
352	forever. This is by design (historical record), but it
353	means the bot repo has no analog of `cargo clean`:
354	code-repo working bytes can be reclaimed (build artifacts
355	come back on `cargo build`); bot-repo bytes are permanent.
356	
357	Sizing assumptions for shared-central scaling are a
358	one-way ratchet, not a steady-state cost.
359	
360	### Mitigation menu (when thresholds approach)
361	
362	None worth building preemptively. Listed so trigger points
363	have known responses:
364	
365	1. **Bot-repo pruning/archiving.** Move closed-out
366	   sessions (older than N months, or tied to commits no
367	   longer in any active branch) to an archive repo.
368	   Active bot repo stays small; archive fetched on demand
369	   when older history is referenced. The bot thinks this
370	   is the cheapest big win — recent sessions are the hot
371	   set, old ones are cold.
372	2. **Sparse fetch by trailer-referenced sessions.** When
373	   fetching the code repo, derive the set of bot-side
374	   change_ids referenced by ochids in the fetched range
375	   and fetch only those bot commits. Requires git/jj
376	   sparse-fetch + a trailer-walking step. Heavier to
377	   build but decouples clone cost from bot-repo size.
378	3. **Per-user bot repos.** The structural alternative —
379	   see "Per-user vs shared bot repo" above. Viable when
380	   shared-central pruning/sparse-fetch isn't enough.
381	4. **LFS for `.jsonl`.** Probably overkill — LFS targets
382	   binary blobs, not append-only text. Listed only
383	   because it's a known git escape hatch.
384	
385	### Tracking trigger
386	
387	Concretely measurable: the 311M baseline is the current
388	benchmark. When the shared-central bot repo crosses ~1G
389	or starts dominating clone time noticeably, that's the
390	design trigger for pruning. User count alone is not the
391	right signal; bot-repo size is.
392	
393	## Future-direction notes
394	
395	Two alternatives to the partner-bookmark technique that may
396	be worth considering once we live with the current scheme:
397	
398	### Always keep the bot repo on main
399	
400	Instead of creating a partner bookmark on the bot side for
401	each code-side branch, the bot side could be locked to a
402	single bookmark — `main` — for the entire lifetime of the
403	project. Every push would advance bot main, regardless of
404	what bookmark the code side is on.
405	
406	The bot thinks this aligns with "bot history is one linear
407	journal" more strongly than partner bookmarks do. Partner
408	bookmarks introduce *parallel* bot bookmarks even though the
409	bot history under them is still linear; an always-main
410	discipline removes that parallelism entirely.
411	
412	Trade: requires push / finalize / sync to support asymmetric
413	bookmark names per side (see next note).
414	
415	### Multi-bookmark-name support in push / finalize / sync
416	
417	Today, `vc-x1 push <bookmark>` advances the same bookmark
418	name on both code and bot sides. The "always-on-main"
419	discipline above (and other asymmetric workflows) requires
420	push / finalize / sync to take separate bookmark arguments
421	per side, e.g.:
422	
423	```
424	vc-x1 push --code-bookmark init-clone-refactor --bot-bookmark main
425	vc-x1 push -B init-clone-refactor              # bot defaults to main
426	vc-x1 push init-clone-refactor                  # both, today's behavior
427	```
428	
429	The bot thinks the right shape lands once `--scope` is
430	fully wired across the dual-repo commands — `--scope`
431	already handles "which side does this command apply to,"
432	and adding per-side bookmark arguments is a natural
433	extension.
434	
435	## Open questions worth thinking about
436	
437	- **Conflict resolution sessions: how do they get
438	  authored?** When user C resolves a merge conflict, does
439	  their bot session start a fresh chain rooted "from
440	  nowhere," or branch off one of the contributing chains?
441	  The chain-rooting question affects how the viewer renders
442	  it.
443	- **De-duplication on octopus merges.** If parents share
444	  ancestry, you'll get redundant ochids. Trim to unique
445	  tips? Keep all? The bot thinks trim, but worth deciding.
446	- **Bot-side bookmark scheme.** Per-session bookmark?
447	  Per-user-per-feature? Naming convention matters because
448	  bookmarks are how `jj git push` decides what to send.
449	- **Viewer concerns.** "Show me the sessions that
450	  contributed to this commit" is straightforward (read the
451	  trailers, walk back in each chain to the relevant range).
452	  "Show me the code commits this session contributed to" is
453	  the inverse and needs an index, since change_ids don't
454	  carry forward references. Probably build the index from a
455	  one-time scan of code-side trailers.
456	- **Migration story for existing repos** with single-ochid
457	  trailers — they're already valid in the multi-ochid
458	  scheme (N=1 case), so probably no migration needed. Worth
459	  confirming on the parser side.
460	
```

Done — added a `### Staged fork integration` subsection to `notes/forks-multi-user.md` (under `## Multi-user collaboration`). It captures the two genuinely new pieces:
- The staged workflow: as-is acceptance branch (ochids preserved verbatim) → merge/squash/tweak as separate cycles on an intermediate branch → integrated commit carries the resulting ochid *set*.
- The third ochid representation — `.vc-config.toml` repo-index tuple `(repo-index, chid)` — alongside the path and URL forms the file already documents, with the indirection trade-off noted.

The file already covered multi-line ochids, URL-shaped ochids, and per-user-vs-shared, so I cross-linked rather than duplicated.

## On Point 1 — one protocol

Agree, and it's the right call. There's **one protocol** — the cycle: Preparation / Work-N / Close-out, nested as needed. **push** and **squash** are not modes baked in at cycle start — they're discretionary actions:
- **push** — when you want a backup (durability) or to publish interim work (visibility).
- **squash** — when you want to tidy history, typically before a publishing push.

So "single-push / multi-push" disappear as mode names. The skeleton's "two modes" subsection becomes a short "push & squash are discretionary" note instead.

**One thing that needs your explicit confirm** — this changes an existing CLAUDE.md rule. `## Commit-Push-Finalize Flow` currently *mandates* "Run this flow after every step — dev markers land on the remote as they happen." Under discretionary push that mandate relaxes to a guideline: "push to back up or to publish." The **close-out push stays mandatory** — the cycle's result must be published — only *interim* pushes become your call. Confirm you want that mandate relaxed and I'll fold it into the rewrite.

Consequences that just follow (no decision needed): `.claude` commits once per push; an ochid set on a pushed code commit has one entry per code commit in that push.

Confirm the push-mandate relaxation and I'll do the revised CLAUDE.md rewrite as node 2.
